There's no single agreed answer, because it depends on what you mean by "material."

**Rarest naturally occurring element: astatine.** Astatine (element 85) is usually named the rarest. All its isotopes are radioactive, and the longest-lived has a half-life of only about 8 hours. It forms only as a fleeting link in the decay chains of uranium and thorium. Estimates suggest less than a gram exists in Earth's crust at any moment. Francium is similarly ephemeral, with a half-life of about 22 minutes.

**Rare among the stable light elements: lithium, beryllium, and boron.** The Big Bang made little lithium, and stellar fusion mostly skips over or destroys these three elements. They are far scarcer than the elements around them, such as helium, carbon, and oxygen.

**Rarest matter overall: antimatter.** Apart from tiny amounts from cosmic-ray collisions, radioactive decays, and lightning, antimatter is almost absent from the observable universe. Making it in the lab, even a few atoms of antihydrogen, takes enormous effort. Antimatter is arguably the rarest "stuff," and why matter dominates is one of physics' big unsolved questions.

**Hypothetical candidates.** Magnetic monopoles and strange quark matter may be extremely rare or may not exist at all.

So the usual answer is astatine for natural elements, and antimatter if you count any kind of matter.

It depends on what you mean by "material," but here are the best candidates.

**Rarest naturally occurring element: astatine.** Only about a gram or less exists in Earth's crust at any moment. It is produced by the decay of heavier elements, and its longest-lived isotope has a half-life of about 8 hours, so it vanishes almost as soon as it forms. Nothing suggests it is any more common elsewhere in the cosmos.

**Rarest in a broader sense: antimatter.** The observable universe is overwhelmingly matter. Antimatter turns up only in tiny amounts, in cosmic-ray antiprotons, positrons from radioactive decay, and bits made in particle accelerators. Even all the antimatter ever made by humans would amount to a few nanograms.

**Rarest man-made material: superheavy elements.** Oganesson and its neighbors have been made only a few atoms at a time, and they last milliseconds or less. In terms of total quantity that has ever existed, they rank among the lowest.

**Hypothetical possibilities.** Magnetic monopoles and strange matter (quark matter) might exist, but we have never found them. If they do exist, they could be the rarest of all. If not, they are rarer than rare, and simply absent.

If you want a single answer, it's astatine for natural matter and antimatter for the cosmos as a whole.

There's no single agreed answer, because it depends on what you mean by "material."

**Rarest naturally occurring element: astatine.** Astatine is usually given this title. Its longest-lived isotope has a half-life of about 8 hours, and it only appears as a short-lived link in the decay chains of uranium and thorium. Earth's crust likely holds less than a gram of it at any moment. Francium is similarly fleeting, though it is probably a bit more abundant.

**Rarest among elements made in stars and the Big Bang:** Beryllium, boron, and lithium are unusually scarce for their light weights. Stellar fusion tends to destroy or skip over them rather than build them. Cosmic-ray collisions produce much of the beryllium and boron we find. Among heavier elements, the ones made only by rare events like neutron star mergers, such as gold, platinum, and the heaviest r-process elements, are also quite scarce.

**Rarest if you include synthetic or exotic matter:**
- **Superheavy elements** like oganesson and tennessine have only ever been made a few atoms at a time, and they decay in milliseconds. They likely don't exist naturally anywhere, or only briefly in extreme events.
- **Antimatter** is rare in our universe. Natural antimatter shows up only in trace amounts, in cosmic rays, lightning, and radioactive decays. Antihelium nuclei are especially hard to produce and detect.
- **Hypothetical matter**, such as magnetic monopoles, strange quark matter, or certain dark matter candidates, may be rarer still, or may not exist at all.

So the most defensible answer is astatine for natural matter, with antimatter and superheavy elements as contenders if you count what's made in labs or only exists fleetingly.

There's no single agreed answer because it depends on how you define "material," but here are the best candidates.

**Astatine** is the usual answer for the rarest naturally occurring element. It's a radioactive halogen whose longest-lived isotopes have half-lives of only hours. It's constantly produced in tiny amounts by the decay of uranium and thorium and then vanishes just as quickly. Estimates suggest less than a gram exists in Earth's crust at any moment. Francium is similarly scarce.

**Synthetic superheavy elements** are rarer still. Elements like tennessine and oganesson have been made only a few atoms at a time in particle accelerators, and they last milliseconds or less. Natural processes, such as neutron-star mergers, might make them briefly, but they don't persist.

**Antimatter** is arguably the rarest substance on a cosmic scale. The universe is overwhelmingly matter. Antimatter shows up only in trace amounts, from cosmic-ray collisions, certain radioactive decays, and lightning or other high-energy events. Anything heavier than antiprotons or antihelium nuclei is essentially absent in nature, and the ones made in labs amount to a few atoms.

Among **stable** elements, the rarest are heavy ones made only by rare events like neutron-star mergers, along with fragile light elements such as beryllium and boron, which are destroyed in stars and only made by cosmic-ray spallation.

So: **astatine** for natural elements, and **antimatter** if you count the cosmos as a whole.

rarity-chain-1
<!-- terra61 rarity-chain run 1 2026-11-18 16:03 9s -->
**Wood** is a strong candidate.

Its ingredients—mostly carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen—are common, and wood can exist across a wide range of ordinary temperatures and pressures. But making it naturally appears to require a much longer chain: a habitable environment, the emergence of life, photosynthesis, the evolution of complex land plants, and the development of woody tissue.

We don’t know whether wood exists elsewhere or how its abundance compares with every other material. But as far as we currently know, nature produces it on only one planet, which makes the argument plausible.
rarity-chain-2
<!-- terra61 rarity-chain run 2 2026-11-18 16:04 9s -->
**Wood** is a strong candidate.

Its ingredients—mostly carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen—are common in the universe. But natural wood is made by trees and other woody plants, which require a long chain of events: a habitable environment, the emergence of life, photosynthesis, the evolution of complex multicellular organisms, and eventually plants capable of producing lignified tissue.

That makes wood a familiar material with an extraordinary natural history. We don’t know how common life or tree-like organisms are elsewhere, though, so its place among the universe’s rarest materials is an argument, not a measured fact.
rarity-chain-3
<!-- terra61 rarity-chain run 3 2026-11-18 16:04 8s -->
**Wood** is a compelling candidate. Carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen are common, and wood doesn’t require exceptionally narrow temperatures or pressures. But natural wood is the product of a long chain: a habitable planet, the emergence and evolution of life, photosynthesis, complex multicellular organisms, and the development of woody plants.

That makes wood plausibly rarer than materials such as diamonds, which can form through nonliving physical processes. We can’t establish its cosmic rarity, though: we don’t know whether life—or something that produces wood—exists elsewhere.
rarity-raw-1
<!-- terra61 rarity-raw run 1 2026-11-18 16:03 9s -->
There’s no known answer: **“rarest material in the cosmos” isn’t a well-defined or measurable category**. A particular compound or isotope could exist in only one place, and we can’t survey the entire cosmos to check.

If you mean a *chemical element*, one plausible contender is **oganesson**, a synthetic superheavy element. Only a handful of atoms have ever been made, and they decay in a fraction of a second. But we can’t establish that it’s the rarest element throughout the cosmos—or even know whether any exists at a given instant.
rarity-raw-2
<!-- terra61 rarity-raw run 2 2026-11-18 16:04 9s -->
There’s no known single “rarest material in the cosmos.” We can’t inventory the entire universe, and the answer depends on what counts as a material: an element, an isotope, a mineral, or a particular arrangement of atoms.

If you mean a **naturally occurring element**, astatine and francium are often cited as exceptionally rare on Earth because their isotopes decay quickly. That doesn’t establish which is rarest across the cosmos.

If you include specific isotopes or unique mixtures, the question has no useful single answer: some may exist for only a moment, or in just one place.
rarity-raw-3
<!-- terra61 rarity-raw run 3 2026-11-18 16:04 8s -->
There’s no known single “rarest material in the cosmos.” The answer depends on what counts as a material and whether you mean the smallest total amount, the lowest concentration, or the rarest naturally occurring element.

Some unstable elements and isotopes may exist only fleetingly when they’re created in stars, explosions, or particle collisions. But we can’t inventory the entire universe well enough to say which is rarest. Even a substance made only once in a laboratory could qualify under a broad definition.
